[269:50.32] So what is a transformer? Transformers
[269:53.28] operate on a concept called sequencetose
[269:55.52] sequence learning. Essentially, they
[269:58.00] take a sequence of tokens as an input
[270:00.16] and predict the next token in the
[270:02.00] output. A great example of this is
[270:04.32] language translation. Imagine inputting
[270:07.20] good morning in English and the
[270:09.12] transformer process this and outputs the
[270:11.28] translation in languages like Japanese,
[270:14.00] Korean or German. The key is how it
[270:17.28] efficiently process the relationship
[270:19.04] between words. Since we know what a
[270:21.36] transformer is, let's dig a bit deep
[270:23.84] about them. A transformer has two
[270:26.56] primary components encoder and decoder.
[270:30.00] Encoder identifies relationship between
[270:32.16] parts of the input sequence. Whereas the
[270:34.32] decoder uses these relationships to
[270:36.40] generate the output sequence. This
[270:38.64] division is what allows transformers to
[270:40.72] handle task like text translation or
[270:43.04] summarizations with remarkable accuracy.
[270:46.08] Now that we have the idea of
[270:47.60] transformers, let's discuss how they
[270:49.76] evolve. Before transformers, they were
[270:52.56] other neural networks like RNN,
[270:54.96] recurrent neural networks invented by
[270:57.20] David Rumlh in 1986.
[271:00.32] However, RNN's face significant
[271:02.64] challenges. They would forget early
[271:04.72] parts of the sequence as they processed
[271:06.72] longer ones and couldn't handle
[271:08.64] dependencies efficiently. Additionally,
[271:11.52] RNN relied on recurrence which made them
[271:14.32] inefficient and incapable of
[271:16.32] parallelization.
[271:18.00] Then came long short-term memory
[271:20.40] introduced by Howeter and Smith Huber in
[271:23.68] 1997.
[271:25.60] Long shortterm memory improved by
[271:27.92] remembering sequences for a longer
[271:30.00] duration and addressing some of the
[271:31.76] memory issues in RNN.
[271:34.24] However, they were slow to train and
[271:36.32] difficult to manage at scale. Finally,
[271:39.12] transformers transformed neural
[271:40.96] networks. First introduced in the
[271:42.88] landmark paper, attention is all you
[271:45.36] need. Transformers addressed all the
[271:47.76] problems faced by RNN and LSTMs. They
[271:51.20] used a completely attention-based
[271:53.20] mechanism, eliminating reliance on
[271:55.60] recurrence. This made transformers
[271:58.00] capable of remembering context
[271:59.76] efficiently, training faster and being
[272:02.64] parallelized, enabling multitasking and
[272:05.52] significantly speeding up processes. Now
[272:08.24] let's discuss on the attention
[272:09.92] mechanism. Think about the sentence.
[272:12.72] This cat wants to jump on the box. The
[272:15.44] attention mechanism identifies the most
[272:17.92] relevant parts of this sentence like
[272:21.04] cat, jump and box and focuses on these
[272:24.96] elements while processing the data. Now
[272:27.92] that we know how transformers have
[272:29.68] evolved, now let's discuss their
[272:31.84] architecture. A transformer consists of
[272:34.64] two main components, an encoder and a
[272:37.04] decoder, each typically consisting six
[272:39.76] layers. Inside the encoder there is one
[272:42.16] attention layer and one feed forward
[272:44.56] layer. While the decoder contains two
[272:46.72] attention layers and one feed forward
[272:48.72] layer. The magic of parallelism comes
[272:51.44] from how data is fed into the network.
[272:54.08] In the attention layer, all the words
[272:56.24] are processed simultaneously with each
[272:58.80] word forming combinations with other in
[273:00.96] the sentence. This allows the model to
[273:03.36] capture relationships and context
[273:05.36] efficiently. After processing in the
[273:07.92] attention layer, the data is sent to the
[273:09.84] feed forward layer where it is learned
[273:12.48] layer by layer. The input to the encoder
[273:15.04] and decoder are the raw input embeddings
[273:17.68] which are numerical representation of
[273:19.60] words. On top of these embeddings,
[273:21.84] positional encodings are added to help
[273:23.92] the model understand the position and
[273:26.16] the order of each word in the sequence.
[273:28.96] If we simplify embeddings, there are
[273:31.20] essentially vector representation of
[273:33.12] words in an n dimensional space. At the
[273:35.76] top of the architecture, there are two
[273:37.68] layers of the output probabilities
[273:39.60] converting the final output into the
[273:41.60] form of human can understand. These
[273:44.24] inputs are represented as vectors with
[273:46.88] their length corresponding to the size
[273:48.64] of vocabulary.
[273:50.96] Now what truly makes transformer unique
[273:53.44] is the inclusion of normalization layers
[273:56.32] which normalize the output from sub
[273:58.32] layers. Additionally, skip connections,
[274:01.04] the dark arrows in the architecture,
[274:03.28] forward critical information that
[274:05.04] bypasses self attention or fed forward
[274:07.36] layers directly to the normalization
[274:09.52] layers. This ensures the model does not
[274:12.08] forget important details and effectively
[274:14.48] passes vital information further into
[274:16.56] the network. Now moving forward, let's
[274:19.60] discuss why transformers are important.
[274:22.32] Transformers are vital because they
[274:24.32] utilize semi-supervised learning. They
[274:26.72] are trained on massive unlabelled data
[274:28.56] sets enabling them to generalize across
[274:31.04] a wide range of task. Unlike older
[274:33.84] models, transformers don't need to
[274:35.60] process data sequentially. Their
[274:37.52] attention mechanism allow them to focus
[274:39.68] on the most relevant context which
[274:41.92] significantly speeds up training.
[274:43.92] Transformers revolutionize data
[274:45.92] processing by eliminating the need to
[274:47.76] handle data sequentially allowing for
[274:50.32] parallel processing and significantly
[274:52.48] enhancing efficiency. The attention
[274:54.80] mechanism lies at the core of
[274:56.48] transformers, enabling the model to
[274:58.48] focus on the most relevant parts of the
[275:00.56] input sequence and improving accuracy
[275:03.12] and understanding of context.
[275:05.28] Furthermore, transformers excel at
[275:07.44] providing context, ensuring that the
[275:09.68] meaning of each word or token is
[275:11.76] accurately interpreted within its
[275:13.68] surroundings. Lastly, these models
[275:16.48] dramatically speeds up the training
[275:18.32] process, making them faster and more
[275:20.80] efficient compared to traditional neural
[275:22.80] networks, thus redefining AI's
[275:25.12] capabilities across diverse
[275:26.72] applications. Now that we know why
